(0,h)
E^{0,v}_{h}

2

The half-ellipse of semi-major axis of length
h
, passing through the intersection of two birds, passes through their heads, for any pair of birds. For
(0,h)
to be a handover point, the regions
E^{v}_{h}
must be empty of heads.

Figure 4.6
.
Half-ball condition and half-ellipse condition.

Remark 4.20
.

We often refer to the key geometric result, Lemma
4.18
, as the
half-ball condition
in case of
v=1
.

Discussion 4.21

(
Speed
v\neq 1
)
.

Consider the general setup where the stations are moving at speed
v\neq 1
. Then the region that needs to be empty
of head points, for a point
(0,h)
to be on the lower envelope process, is the region enclosed by the upper half
ellipse with equation
v^{2}x^{2}+y^{2}=h^{2}
, see Figure
4.6
(Picture
62
).
For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
E^{s,v}_{h}
the open set enclosed by the upper half-ellipse
given by the equation
v^{2}(x-s)^{2}+y^{2}=h^{2}
and
E^{v}_{h}:=E^{0,v}_{h}
. ​
The probability that
E^{s,v}_{h}
is empty of head points from
{\mathcal{H}}
is

\mathbb{P}\left({\mathcal{H}}(E^{s,v}_{h})=0\right)=e^{-2\lambda v|E^{s,v}_{h}|}=e^{-2\lambda v\times\pi\frac{h^{2}}{2v}}=e^{-\lambda\pi h^{2}},

using Remark
4.5
. We refer to Lemma
4.18
as the
half-ellipse condition

in the case of
v\neq 1
. Similarly, we can generalize this to the multi-speed case, which will be the content of
Section
7
.

Remark 4.22
.

The void probability remains the same as in the unit speed case. In the fast movement case
(v>1)
,
the increase in the intensity of heads is compensated by the region of the upper half ellipse having smaller area.
In the slow case
(v<1)
, the decrease in the intensity of heads is balanced by the area of a larger upper half ellipse. In this case, we denote the lower envelope as
{\mathcal{L}}_{e}(v)
.

Remark 4.23
.

We say that an intersection point
(s,u)
of two radial birds, does not correspond to a handover if
(s,u)\in{\mathcal{L}}^{+}_{e}
. For speed
v\neq 1
, we denote the open region above the lower envelope as
{\mathcal{L}}^{+}_{e}(v)
.

4.5.
Construction of the handover point process

This subsection showcases how the handover point process can be derived from the lower envelope process, more precisely using the marked head point process
{\mathcal{H}}_{c}
with set valued marks defined in (
4.14
). The construction of the handover point process
{\mathcal{V}}
from

{\mathcal{H}}_{c}
is based on finding pairs of head points corresponding to a handover epoch, along with a non-negative mark attached to them.
The former represents the time-coordinate at which the two radial birds intersect and the mark represents
the height at which the corresponding birds intersect. A thinning is used to retain only those pairs of heads that gives rise to handovers.

By Remark
4.12
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{c}
is defined using the head point process

{\mathcal{H}}
along with the corresponding radial bird attached to each head as a mark. By Property (
2
), each of the closed set valued mark is determined by the coordinate of head point. Also the intersection point of two birds
is a function of the pair of heads of the corresponding birds. For this reason, we can detect the handovers just using the head point process. We adopt the convention to denote the radial bird with it’s head
at
(t,h)
by
C_{(t,h)}
, in view of Property
2
.

t
h
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\bigtimes
\bigtimes
\bigtimes
\bigtimes

Figure 4.7
.
Construction of the handover point process
{\mathcal{V}}
and its marked version
\hat{\mathcal{V}}
.

Let
(t,h)\neq(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})\in\mathbb{H}^{+}
be such that
t^{\prime}<t
and the corresponding radial birds
C_{(t,h)},C_{(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}

intersect at
(\hat{s},\hat{h})
. The quantities
\hat{s}
and
\hat{h}

are derived later in (
4.22
) and (
4.23
), respectively, in terms of
(t,h),(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})
. Let
A(\hat{s},\hat{h})
be the event that the intersection point
(\hat{s},\hat{h})
represents a handover. By Lemma
4.18
, the event
A(\hat{s},\hat{h})
is equivalent to having no points of
{\mathcal{H}}
in the upper half-ellipse
U_{\hat{h}}^{\hat{s}}
, i.e.,
\{{\mathcal{H}}(U_{\hat{h}}^{\hat{s}})=0\}
. In this sense the point process given by

(4.16)

\hat{\mathcal{V}}:=\sum_{(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{H}}}\;\;\sum_{(T_{j},H_{j})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,\mbox{:}\,T_{j}<T_{i}}\delta_{(\hat{S},\hat{H})}\,\mathbbmss{1}_{A(\hat{S},\hat{H})},

represents the collection of intersection points responsible for handovers, where
(\hat{S},\hat{H})
is the intersection point of the radial birds at
(T_{i},H_{i})
and
(T_{j},H_{j})
. We have considered the pair of head points
(T_{i},H_{i})
and
(T_{j},H_{j})
such that
T_{j}<T_{i}
, just to avoid any double counting. In Figure
4.7
, the points
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\times},\times\}
are the projections of intersections of the birds, on the time axis, representing the corresponding pair of head points. We shall discard the pair of heads corresponding to the black ones (
\times
) and retain only the pair for blue ones
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\times})
to form the handover point process. The blue nodes (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}
) correspond to the handover epochs and the distances at which the handovers happen. Finally,
the handover point process is given by

(4.17)

{\mathcal{V}}:=\sum_{(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{H}}}\;\;\sum_{(T_{j},H_{j})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,\mbox{:}\,T_{j}<T_{i}}\delta_{\hat{S}}\,\mathbbmss{1}_{A(\hat{S},\hat{H})}.

We call the point process
\hat{\mathcal{V}}
the marked version of the handover point process
{\mathcal{V}}
, where the handover points are associated to non-negative marks that represents the height at which the corresponding radial birds intersect. In another words, the mark denotes the handover distance.
The mark
\hat{H}
is also used in for
the retention of the pair of head points corresponding to the intersection at
(\hat{S},\hat{H})
.

Remark 4.24
.

Alternatively, in view of (
4.17
) one can define the handover point process
{\mathcal{V}}
as

(4.18)

{\mathcal{V}}:=\sum_{(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{H}}}\;\;\sum_{(T_{j},H_{j})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,\mbox{:}\,T_{j}>T_{i}}\delta_{\hat{S}}\,\mathbbmss{1}_{A(\hat{S},\hat{H})},

by counting pair of head points
(T_{i},H_{i}),(T_{j},H_{j})\in{\mathcal{H}}
such that
T_{j}>T_{i}
.

In the following result, the time-stationarity of the handover point process
{\mathcal{V}}
, is inherited from the same property of the head point process
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Lemma 4.25

(Time-stationarity of
{\mathcal{V}}
)
.

The handover point process
{\mathcal{V}}
is stationary with respect to time.

4.6.
Handover frequency for the single-speed

Theorem 4.26

(Handover frequency for the single-speed)
.

The handover frequency or intensity of the
handover point process
{\mathcal{V}}
is
\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}=\frac{4v\sqrt{\lambda}}{\pi}
, where
\lambda
is
the intensity of mobile stations and v is their speed.

The proof relies on the representation of the
two-point Palm probability distribution of
{\mathcal{H}}
, which we define in the following before going into the proof of the last theorem.

4.6.1.
Two point Palm probability distribution of
{\mathcal{H}}

It is clear from (
4.16
) that each handover event is characterized as an intersection of
two radial bird particles, that correspond to two atoms of the head point process
{\mathcal{H}}
satisfying
certain properties. This requires us to define the two-point Palm probability measure for
{\mathcal{H}}
. Recall that, from Lemma
4.3
, the head point process
{\mathcal{H}}
is Poisson with intensity measure
\nu
having for density
{\rm d}\nu={\rm d}t\otimes 2\lambda{\rm d}h
.

Let
M(\mathbb{H}^{+})
be the space of all locally finite measures on
\mathbb{H}^{+}
. We define
{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbb{H}^{+})
as the
\sigma
-algebra on
M(\mathbb{H}^{+})
, generated by maps of the form
\mu\mapsto\mu(A)
, for any
A\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{H}^{+})
and
\mu\in M(\mathbb{H}^{+})
, where
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{H}^{+})
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathbb{H}^{+}
, see
[
3
]
for more details.
The following result is classical for Poisson point processes:

Lemma 4.27

(Two-point Palm probability distribution of
{\mathcal{H}}
)
.

The two point Palm probability distribution of the head point process is

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{(t,h),(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}(B):=\mathbb{P}\left({\mathcal{H}}^{(t,h),(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}\in B\right),{}

for any
B\in{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbb{H}^{+})
, with
{\mathcal{H}}^{(t,h),(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}:={\mathcal{H}}+\delta_{(t,h)}+\delta_{(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}

and
{\mathcal{H}}
the stationary version of the head point process.

This result extends naturally to the marked version of the head point process
{\mathcal{H}}_{c}
defined in (
4.14
).

Lemma 4.28

(Two-point Palm probability distribution of
{\mathcal{H}}_{c}
)
.

The two point Palm probability distribution of
{\mathcal{H}}_{c}
is

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{c}}^{(t,h,C),(t^{\prime},h^{\prime},C^{\prime})}(\,\cdot\,):=\mathbb{P}\left({\mathcal{H}}_{c}^{(t,h,C),(t^{\prime},h^{\prime},C^{\prime})}\in\cdot\,\right),{}

with
{\mathcal{H}}_{c}^{(t,h,C),(t^{\prime},h^{\prime},C^{\prime})}:={\mathcal{H}}_{c}+\delta_{(t,h,C)}+\delta_{(t^{\prime},h^{\prime},C^{\prime})}

and
{\mathcal{H}}_{c}
the stationary version of the head point process.
Here
C
and
C^{\prime}
are the radial birds with head point at
(t,h)
and
(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})
, respectively.

Suppose there are two radial bird particles
C,C^{\prime}
with their heads at
(t,h),(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})
, respectively.
Let
(\hat{s},\hat{h})
be the intersection point of
C,C^{\prime}
,
where
\hat{s}
is the time when the two radial birds intersect and
\hat{h}
is the distance at which they intersect.
Let
U_{\hat{h}}^{\hat{s}}
be the upper half-ball centered at
(\hat{s},0)
and of radius
\hat{h}
.
It follows from Lemma
4.18
and Remark
4.19
that

\partial^{*}U_{\hat{h}}^{\hat{s}}
also passes through both heads
(t,h)
and
(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})
,
see Figure
4.8
. It also follows from Lemma
4.18
that
a handover happens at
(\hat{s},\hat{h})
if and only if
{\mathcal{H}}(U_{\hat{h}}^{\hat{s}})=0
.

t
(\hat{s},0)
(\hat{s},\hat{h})
\bullet
(t,h)
C
\bullet
(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})
C^{\prime}

Figure 4.8
.
Given
(t,h)
and
(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})
, the intersection
(\hat{s},\hat{h})
is a handover point if and only if
{\mathcal{H}}(U_{\hat{h}}^{\hat{s}})=0
.

We have the following result about the independence of the head point processes restricted to

U_{\hat{h}}^{\hat{s}}
and
(U_{\hat{h}}^{\hat{s}})^{c}
, under the two point Palm probability measure

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{c}}^{(t,h,C),(t^{\prime},h^{\prime},C^{\prime})}
. We state it as a corollary without proof, as it directly
follows from the fact that Poisson point process on disjoint sets are independent, together with Lemma
4.18
.

Corollary 4.29
.

Under the two-point Palm probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{(t,h),(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}
,

(i)

The head point processes
{\mathcal{H}}|_{U^{\hat{s}}_{\hat{h}}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}|_{(U^{\hat{s}}_{\hat{h}})^{c}}
are independent.

(ii)

Additionally, under the event
\{{\mathcal{H}}(U^{\hat{s}}_{\hat{h}})=0\}
, the point process

{\mathcal{H}}|_{(U^{\hat{s}}_{\hat{h}})^{c}}
is Poisson with intensity of density
{\rm d}t\otimes 2\lambda{\rm d}h
.

Proof of Theorem
4.26
(Handover frequency).

The proof mainly relies on the characterization
for a point to be on the lower envelope
{\mathcal{L}}_{e}
in terms of void probability of half-balls (Lemma
4.18
).
Let
X_{i},X_{j}
be the initial locations of two stations and
(R_{i},\alpha_{i})
,
(R_{j},\alpha_{j})
are corresponding
to
X_{i},X_{j}
. Let
f((R_{i},\alpha_{i}),\cdot)
and
f((R_{j},\alpha_{j}),\cdot)
be the curves of two radial birds

C_{i},C_{j}
, with their heads at
(T_{i},H_{i})
and
(T_{j},H_{j})
, respectively. For
(T_{i},H_{i})
and
(T_{j},H_{j})
with
T_{j}<T_{i}
, let
(S_{i,j},H_{i,j})
be the intersection point of the associated radial birds.
We will see later that
(S_{i,j},H_{i,j})
can be expressed in terms of
(T_{i},H_{i})
and
(T_{j},H_{j})
.
For any
(s,h)\in\mathbb{H}^{+}
, define

(4.19)

A(s,h):=\{(s,h)\text{ is a handover point}\}.

We can write for the event
A(S_{i,j},H_{i,j})(\equiv A_{i,j})
that,
\mathbbmss{1}_{A_{i,j}}\equiv\mathbbmss{1}_{A_{i,j}}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
. Then the handover frequency can be written as

(4.20)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}=m_{{\mathcal{V}}}[0,1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,\mbox{:}\,T_{i}\in[0,1]}\;\;\sum_{(T_{j},H_{j})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,\mbox{:}\,T_{j}<T_{i}}\mathbbmss{1}_{A_{i,j}}\right],

based on the construction of the handover point process in Subsection
4.5
, in particular at Equation (
4.16
). The last expression clearly suggests using now the factorial power of order
2
of
{\mathcal{H}}
.
The point process
{\mathcal{H}}
being Poisson, any factorial power can be expressed in terms of a product measure.
More precisely, by applying the multivariate Campbell-Mecke formula for the factorial power of order 2 of
{\mathcal{H}}
, i.e.,
{\mathcal{H}}^{2,\neq}
(Proposition 2.3.24 and Proposition 2.3.25, in
[
3
]
), we get that

\displaystyle\lambda_{{\mathcal{V}}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,\mbox{:}\,T_{i}\in[0,1]}\;\;\sum_{(T_{j},H_{j})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,\mbox{:}\,T_{j}<T_{i}}\mathbbmss{1}_{A_{i,j}}\right]{}

\displaystyle=(2\lambda)^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{t_{1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}^{(t_{1},h_{1}),(t_{2},h_{2})}_{{\mathcal{H}}}\left[\mathbbmss{1}_{A(s,h)}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)\right]\,{\rm d}h_{2}\,{\rm d}t_{2}\,{\rm d}h_{1}\,{\rm d}t_{1}{}

(4.21)

\displaystyle=4\lambda^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{t_{1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{H}}\left[\mathbbmss{1}_{A(s,h)}\left({\mathcal{H}}+\delta_{(t_{1},h_{1})}+\delta_{(t_{2},h_{2})}\right)\right]\,{\rm d}h_{2}\,{\rm d}t_{2}\,{\rm d}h_{1}\,{\rm d}t_{1},

where
(s,h)
is the point that corresponds to the
intersection of the radial birds associated with the heads located at
(t_{1},h_{1})
and
(t_{2},h_{2})
.
Here
{\mathcal{H}}^{(t_{1},h_{1}),(t_{2},h_{2})}={\mathcal{H}}+\delta_{(t_{1},h_{1})}+\delta_{(t_{2},h_{2})}
, is the two-point Palm version of

{\mathcal{H}}
and it’s two-point Palm expectation
\mathbb{E}^{(t_{1},h_{1}),(t_{2},h_{2})}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
is
as described in Lemma
4.27
.

The handover point
(s,h)
can be expressed in terms of
(t_{1},h_{1}),(t_{2},h_{2})
, as follows.
There exists a pair of points
(r_{1},\alpha_{1}),(r_{2},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times(-\pi,\pi]
such that

(t_{1},h_{1})=(-r_{1}\cos\alpha_{1},r_{1}|\sin\alpha_{1}|)
and
(t_{2},h_{2})=(-r_{2}\cos\alpha_{2},r_{2}|\sin\alpha_{2}|)
.
Let
f((r_{1},\alpha_{1}),\cdot)
and
f((r_{2},\alpha_{2}),\cdot)
be the curves of the radial birds
C_{1},C_{2}
,
as defined in (
4.3
). The time coordinate of handover
s
satisfies
f((r_{1},\alpha_{1}),s)=f((r_{2},\alpha_{2}),s)
, i.e.,

s^{2}+2sr_{1}\cos\alpha_{1}+r_{1}^{2}=s^{2}+2sr_{2}\cos\alpha_{2}+r_{2}^{2}.

This implies,

(4.22)

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{1}^{2}-r_{2}^{2}}{2(r_{2}\cos\alpha_{2}-r_{1}\cos\alpha_{1})}=\frac{t_{1}^{2}+h_{1}^{2}-t_{2}^{2}-h_{2}^{2}}{2(t_{1}-t_{2})}.

The time coordinate
s
is unique, which comes from the fact that radial birds
C_{1},C_{2}
with different
t_{1},t_{2}

must intersect exactly once, see Property
5
. Substituting the value of
s
from (
4.22
),
we get the height
h
of the intersection point from:

\displaystyle h^{2}

\displaystyle=s^{2}-2st_{1}+r_{1}^{2}{}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4(t_{1}-t_{2})^{2}}\left[(t_{1}^{2}+h_{1}^{2}-t_{2}^{2}-h_{2}^{2})^{2}-4t_{1}(t_{1}-t_{2})(t_{1}^{2}+h_{1}^{2}-t_{2}^{2}-h_{2}^{2})+4(t_{1}^{2}+h_{1}^{2})(t_{1}-t_{2})^{2}\right]{}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4(t_{1}-t_{2})^{2}}\left[(t_{2}-t_{1})^{4}+2(h_{1}^{2}+h_{2}^{2})(t_{1}-t_{2})^{2}+(h_{2}^{2}-h_{1}^{2})^{2}\right]{}

(4.23)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left[(t_{1}-t_{2})^{2}+2(h_{1}^{2}+h_{2}^{2})+\frac{(h_{2}^{2}-h_{1}^{2})^{2}}{(t_{1}-t_{2})^{2}}\right].

Recall that
A(s,h)
is the event that
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
intersect at
(s,h)
, which is a handover point.
A handover happens at
(s,h)
if and only if
{\mathcal{H}}(U^{s}_{h})=0
,
by Lemma
4.18
. Since
{\mathcal{H}}
is a Poisson point process, by the Slivnyak-Mecke theorem, the probability of the event
A(s,h)
in (
4.21
), is

(4.24)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{H}}}\left[\mathbbmss{1}_{A(s,h)}\left({\mathcal{H}}+\delta_{(t_{1},h_{1})}+\delta_{(t_{2},h_{2})}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{{\mathcal{H}}}\left(A(s,h)\right)=\mathbb{P}_{{\mathcal{H}}}\left({\mathcal{H}}(U^{s}_{h})=0\right)=e^{-\lambda\pi h^{2}},

which does not depend on the time coordinate
s
. Using the probability from (
4.24
) in (
4.21
), we obtain that

(4.25)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=4\lambda^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{t_{1}}e^{-\lambda\pi h^{2}}{\rm d}t_{2}\,{\rm d}h_{2}\,{\rm d}h_{1}\,{\rm d}t_{1},

as
t_{2}
lies on the left of
t_{1}
. Since the height
h
is a function of the difference
t_{1}-t_{2}
as seen in (
4.23
),
the integral in (
4.25
) can be converted into an integral in three variables after changing
the variable
t_{1}-t_{2}
to
t
, as follows

(4.26)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}=4\lambda^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda\pi h^{2}}{\rm d}t\,{\rm d}h_{1}\,{\rm d}h_{2}.

Using the expression for
h^{2}
from (
4.23
) and keeping aside the term
{\frac{1}{2}}(h_{1}^{2}+h_{2}^{2})
,
we evaluate the inner-most integral in (
4.26
) only with respect to
t
as

(4.27)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi}{4}\left[t^{2}+\frac{(h_{2}^{2}-h_{1}^{2})^{2}}{t^{2}}\right]}{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi}{2}|h_{2}^{2}-h_{1}^{2}|}\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2\sqrt{\frac{\lambda\pi}{4}}}=e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi}{2}|h_{2}^{2}-h_{1}^{2}|}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}},

where we used the following relation:

(4.28)

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-ax^{2}-\frac{b}{x^{2}}}\,{\rm d}x=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2\sqrt{a}}e^{-2\sqrt{ab}},

for
a=\frac{\lambda\pi}{4}>0
and
b=\frac{\lambda\pi}{4}(h_{2}^{2}-h_{1}^{2})^{2}>0
.
The exact value of the integral in (
4.28
) can be evaluated from the relation

(4.29)

\int e^{-ax^{2}-\frac{b}{x^{2}}}\,{{\rm d}}x=-\frac{\sqrt{\pi}e^{-2\sqrt{ab}}}{4a}\left[e^{4\sqrt{ab}}\mbox{erfc}(\sqrt{a}x+\sqrt{b}/x)+\mbox{erfc}(\sqrt{a}x-\sqrt{b}/x)\right]+c,

for any
a,b>0
and a constant
c
, where

\mbox{erfc}(x):=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{x}^{\infty}e^{-t^{2}}{\rm d}t
,
\mbox{erfc}(\infty)=0
and
\mbox{erfc}(-\infty)=2
. Substituting the resultant from (
4.27
) to the integral (
4.26
), along with an extra factor of
e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi}{2}(h_{1}^{2}+h_{2}^{2})}
,
the right hand side in (
4.26
) turns out to be

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\lambda^{2}}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi}{2}(h_{1}^{2}+h_{2}^{2})}e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi}{2}|h_{2}^{2}-h_{1}^{2}|}{\rm d}h_{1}\,{\rm d}h_{2}{}

\displaystyle=4\lambda^{\frac{3}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi}{2}\left(h_{1}^{2}+h_{2}^{2}+|h_{2}^{2}-h_{1}^{2}|\right)}{\rm d}h_{1}\,{\rm d}h_{2}{}

\displaystyle=4\lambda^{\frac{3}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda\pi\left(h_{1}^{2}\vee h_{2}^{2}\right)}{\rm d}h_{1}\,{\rm d}h_{2}{}

(4.30)

\displaystyle=4\lambda^{\frac{3}{2}}\frac{1}{\lambda\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(x^{2}\vee y^{2})}{\rm d}x\,{\rm d}y=\frac{4\sqrt{\lambda}}{\pi},

since it can be shown that
\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(x^{2}\vee y^{2})}{\rm d}x\,{\rm d}y=1
. This completes the proof of Theorem
4.26
.
∎

Remark 4.30
.

In view of (
4.18
), we can give an alternative definition of the handover frequency, instead of the one in (
4.20
), as follows:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}=m_{{\mathcal{V}}}[0,1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,:\,T_{i}\in[0,1]}\;\;\sum_{(T_{j},H_{j})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,:\,T_{j}>T_{i}}\mathbbmss{1}_{A_{i,j}}\right].{}

Under this definition we can carry out the entire computation of the handover frequency
\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Figure 4.9
.
In the dual system the handover points are represented by the crossings of the Voronoi boundaries by the trajectory of an user moving along a horizontal line.

Remark 4.31

(Speed
v\neq 1
)
.

If the mobile stations move at a general speed
v
, the handover frequency can be computed to be

\lambda_{{\mathcal{V}}}=\frac{4v\sqrt{\lambda}}{\pi}
. This is justified since, in view of Lemma
4.3
and Remark
4.5
,
the speed
v
scales the time axis proportionally.

Remark 4.32
.

We would like to mention that the proof technique developed above, naturally lifts to the multi-speed case,
where the stations are moving at different speeds, which is the main content of Section
7
.
In the present section, we have first analyzed the case of single-speed as a preparation for the multi-speed case.

Remark 4.33

(Comparison with the static scenario)
.

Consider the same model, but with static stations and a mobile user moving along a straight line with speed
v
,
see Figure
4.9
. The static model has been studied in various papers of the literature, for example
[
6
]
,
[
8
]
,

[
7
]
,
[
9
]
. The handover frequency is computed as the intensity of the point process
corresponding to the intersection of an infinite line with the boundaries of cells of the corresponding Poisson-Voronoi tessellation. This uses the contact and cord length distribution of the Poisson-Voronoi tessellation
[
21
]
.
The frequency of handovers in the simple dual static model is also
\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}=\frac{4\sqrt{\lambda}}{\pi}
, as analytically proved in
[
9
]
,
[
17
, 7.22]
and
[
15
]
. This fact will be discussed further in the following, as an alternative proof of Theorem
4.26
.

4.6.2.
Another proof of Theorem
4.26

Proof.

We give here a simpler proof of Theorem
4.26
.
Unfortunately, this simpler approach does not extend to the multi-speed case, which
will centrally rely on the methodology developed for the first proof.

Let us first consider the simpler model with the direction of motion fixed and same for all stations,
let us say
\Theta=\theta
. Then at time
t
, the distance of the mobile station, starting at
X=(|X|,\Psi)
,
from the user is given by,

|X^{t}|:=\left(|X|^{2}+2t|X|\cos\alpha+t^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\left(R^{\prime 2}+2tR^{\prime}\cos\alpha^{\prime}+t^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}:=f((R^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}),t),

where
|X|:=R^{\prime}
and
\alpha^{\prime}\equiv(\Psi-\theta)\pmod{2\pi}
. Collectively the pairs of the form
(R^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
give rise
to a marked Poisson point process
\tilde{\Phi}^{\prime}:=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(R^{\prime}_{i},\alpha^{\prime}_{i})}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times[-\pi,\pi]
.
Subsequently, one can construct the head point process
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}:=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(T^{\prime}_{i},H^{\prime}_{i})}
,
where
(T^{\prime}_{i},H^{\prime}_{i}):=(-R^{\prime}_{i}\cos\alpha^{\prime}_{i},R^{\prime}_{i}|\sin\alpha^{\prime}_{i}|)
and the marked head point process

{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{c}:=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(T^{\prime}_{i},H^{\prime}_{i},C^{\prime}_{i})}
, where
C^{\prime}_{i}:=\{(t,f((R^{\prime}_{i},\alpha^{\prime}_{i}),t))\,\mbox{:}\,t\in\mathbb{R}\}
are the radial birds.
The lower envelope process in this case
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(\cdot)
is defined as

{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(t):=\{(t,L^{\prime}(t))\,\mbox{:}\,t\in\mathbb{R}\},\text{ where, }L^{\prime}(t):=\inf_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f((R^{\prime}_{i},\alpha^{\prime}_{i}),t).

The key observation is that
\alpha^{\prime}
has the same distribution as
\alpha
.
Hence the marked head point processes
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{c}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{c}
, the one in the random direction case
(see the Definition
4.10
), have the same distribution. As a result, the lower envelope process
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(\cdot)
has the same distribution as the one in
the random direction case defined in Definition
4.14
, see Figure
4.2
.

As we have seen in Subsection
4.5
, the handover point process is totally determined
by the lower envelope process
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(\cdot)
of the marked head point process
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{c}
.
Hence, the handover point process has the same intensity in both the random and non-random direction cases.
Having just a single direction of motion
\theta
for all mobile stations is equivalent to the situation where
the user is moving in the
\pi+\theta
direction, while all the mobile stations are static,
as considered in
[
8
]
. There the handover frequency is
\frac{4\sqrt{\lambda}}{\pi}

in the unit speed case. Since the radial bird particle process is oblivious of the direction of motion of the stations,
the handover frequency coincides with the one in the static case.
This completes the second proof of Theorem
4.26
.
∎

Remark 4.34
.

Moving beyond handover frequency, our analysis successfully captures the statistical properties of geometric object of interest, studied in the rest of the article. Note that our results hold for both the dynamic and the dual static model, which are captured together through Figure
3.1
.

4.7.
State at typical times and handover times

Let
L_{s}
be the vertical line
t=s
, for some
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Observe that all the radial birds intersect the vertical line
L_{s}
at certain heights. The point process of interest here, representing the ordered distances of the base stations, is given by the intersection of the birds with this vertical line, see Figure
4.10
. The further away a head point is from
L_{s}
, on the right or left, the higher its intersection with the corresponding radial bird. This gives rise to a point process on
L_{s}
, which can be seen as a point process on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Recall that
\pi_{2}(A)=\{\pi_{2}(x,y):\text{ for all }(x,y)\in A\}
, for any
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, where
\pi_{2}:\mathbb{H}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
is the projection map to the
h
-axis such that
\pi_{2}(x,y)=y
for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{H}^{+}
.

Let
\eta_{s}
be the point process on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
corresponding to the intersection points of all the birds with the vertical line
L_{s}
, i.e.,

\eta_{s}:=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}\,\mbox{:}\,(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{H}}}\delta_{\pi_{2}(C_{i}\cap L_{s})},{}

where
C_{i}
is the radial bird corresponding to
(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{H}}
. The point process
\eta_{s}
gives the distances between the user and the mobile stations at time
s
. We would like to characterize the point processes
\{\eta_{s}\}_{s\in\mathbb{R}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, in particular at any typical time and also at handover times.

t
h
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
L_{s}
s
\bullet
\bullet

Figure 4.10
.
All the radial birds intersects the line
L_{s}
at different heights and creates a point process
\eta_{s}
.

Let
\hat{h}_{s}:=\inf\left(Supp(\eta_{s})\right)
. Then
\hat{h}_{s}
is the distance to the serving station at time
s
, which characterizes the power of the signal received by the user. On the other hand the point process
\eta_{s}-\delta_{\hat{h}_{s}}
enables us to encode the distance of all other non-serving stations, contributing to the power of the interference experienced by the user.

Suppose there is a handover at time
0
, namely consider the system under the Palm probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{0}_{\mathcal{V}}
of handovers (to be determined rigorously in Theorem
5.7
in Section
5
). Consider the point process
\eta_{0}
for which
\inf(Supp(\eta_{0}))=\hat{h}
, say. At a typical handover, there are two stations at equal distance
\hat{h}
. We call
\hat{h}
the typical handover distance and we derive its Palm probability distribution in Subsection
5.2
. In the following, we give a result about the distribution of point process
\eta_{0}-\delta_{\hat{h}}
.

Lemma 4.35
.

Conditioned on
\hat{h}
, under the Palm probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{0}_{\mathcal{V}}
, the point process
\eta_{0}
is a Poisson point process on
[\hat{h},\infty)
with intensity measure
\hat{\mu}
with density
{\rm d}\hat{\mu}:=2\pi\lambda r\,{\rm d}r
.

Proof.

Suppose that, under the Palm probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{0}_{\mathcal{V}}
of handovers, there is a handover at time
0
at a distance
\hat{h}
. Then there exists
(t,h),(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{H}}
such that, the radial birds
C_{(t,h)},C_{(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}
intersects at
(0,\hat{h})
and
{\mathcal{H}}(U^{0}_{\hat{h}})=0
. Since
{\mathcal{H}}
itself is a Poisson point process, we have from Corollary
4.29
, sub point processes
{\mathcal{H}}|_{\overline{U^{0}_{\hat{h}}}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}|_{\left(\overline{U^{0}_{\hat{h}}}\right)^{c}}
which are independent and such that
{\mathcal{H}}|_{\left(\overline{U^{0}_{\hat{h}}}\right)^{c}}
is a Poisson point process. Note that the point process
\eta_{0}-\delta_{\hat{h}}
of the distances of the mobile stations at time
0
is created by all the radial birds with the head point process
{\mathcal{H}}|_{\left(\overline{U^{0}_{\hat{h}}}\right)^{c}}
and
Supp(\eta_{0})\subsetneq[\hat{h},\infty)
, conditioned on
\hat{h}
. Since
{\mathcal{H}}|_{\left(\overline{U^{0}_{\hat{h}}}\right)^{c}}
is an independent Poisson point process, the point process
\eta_{0}-\delta_{\hat{h}}
is a Poisson point process with intensity measure
\hat{\mu}
having density
{\rm d}\hat{\mu}:=2\pi\lambda r\,{\rm d}r
on
[\hat{h},\infty)
, as seen in the radial part of the one at (
4.2
). This completes the proof.
∎

5.
Palm probability with respect to handovers and applications

The goal of this section is to determine the Palm probability distribution w.r.t. the handover epochs.
This is done in Subsection
5.1
, by extending the method used in the proof of Theorem
4.26
. This is then leveraged to determine the Palm distribution of the distance to the stations
involved in the handover and the Palm distribution of the time to the next handover.

5.1.
Palm probability with respect to handovers

In this section, the reference probability space is
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},\mathbb{P})
,
with
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}})
the canonical space of point processes on
\mathbb{H}^{+}
.
So a point
\omega
is a realization of the point process

{\mathcal{H}}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(T_{i},H_{i})}
.
One can equip this measure space with the shift
\{\theta_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
along the time axis.
Its action on
{\mathcal{H}}
is defined by

(5.1)

\theta_{t}({\mathcal{H}})=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(T_{i}-t,H_{i})}.

The law
\mathbb{P}
of the Poisson point process
{\mathcal{H}}
is left invariant by this shift.
This shift is ergodic for
\mathbb{P}
.

Consider the handover point process
{\mathcal{V}}
, as constructed in Subsection
4.5
.
This point process is stationary (
\theta_{t}
-compatible) from Lemma
4.25
and has a positive and finite intensity from Theorem
4.26
.
So one can define the Palm probability w.r.t.
{\mathcal{V}}
on the probability space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}})
, which will be denoted as

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{0}
.

Consider the pairs of points
((t,h),(t^{\prime},h^{\prime}))
of
\mathbb{H}^{+}
, such that
t^{\prime}\leq t
and the condition that

{\mathcal{H}}(U^{\hat{s}}_{\hat{h}})=0
holds, where
(\hat{s},\hat{h})
are the coordinates of the intersection
of the two radial birds with heads at
(t,h)
and
(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})
, respectively.
The sequence of rightmost points of such pairs that are in the support of
{\mathcal{H}}
forms
a stationary (
\theta_{t}
-compatible) point process
{\mathcal{R}}
on
\mathbb{R}
.​ This point process has
a positive and finite intensity.
So one can define the Palm probability w.r.t.
{\mathcal{R}}
(on the probability space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}})
),
which will be denoted as
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathcal{R}}}^{0}
.

t
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

Figure 5.1
.
The bijection between point process
{\mathcal{R}}
and the handover point process
{\mathcal{V}}
via the head point process
{\mathcal{H}}
and the intersection points of the radial birds.

As already explained, there is a bijection between the points of
{\mathcal{R}}

and the points of
{\mathcal{V}}
, that will be denoted by
\beta
, (see Figure
5.1
).
Note that if
((t,h),(t^{\prime},h^{\prime}))
is as above, then
\beta(t)=\hat{s}
, the abscissa of the intersection of the two radial birds with these heads.
The following lemma is obtained from Theorem 6.1.35 in
[
3
]
(which is itself a consequence
of the mass transport principle for stationary point processes):

Lemma 5.1
.

For all non-negative measurable functions
f
on
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}})
,

(5.2)

\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{0}[f({\mathcal{H}})]=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{R}}}^{0}[f(\theta_{\beta(0)}({\mathcal{H}}))].

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

The formula (
5.2
) is a special case of that given in
[
3
, Theorem 6.1.35]
when taking

\Phi={\mathcal{V}}
,
\Phi^{\prime}={\mathcal{R}}
and
g(y,\omega)=f(\omega)\mathbbmss{1}_{\{y=\beta^{-1}(0)\}}
.
∎

Let us denote the intensity of the point process
{\mathcal{R}}
, as
\lambda_{\mathcal{R}}
. Due to the above bijection
\beta
between
{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\mathcal{R}}
in the mass transport principle under the Palm probability measures, we get the equality of intensities:

Corollary 5.2
.

The intensity of the point processes
{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\mathcal{R}}
are equal,
\lambda_{\mathcal{R}}=\lambda_{{\mathcal{V}}}
.

We now use the Poisson structure of
{\mathcal{H}}
to get:

Lemma 5.3
.

For all non-negative measurable functions
f
on
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}})
,

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84544pt\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{R}}}^{0}[f(\theta_{\beta(0)}({\mathcal{H}}))]

\displaystyle=\frac{4\lambda^{2}}{\lambda_{{\mathcal{R}}}}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}}\!\!\!\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{H}}\left[\mathbbmss{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}\left(U^{\hat{s}(0,h,-t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}_{\hat{h}(0,h,-t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}\right)=0}f\left(\theta_{\hat{s}(0,h,-t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}\left({\mathcal{H}}+\delta_{(0,h)}+\delta_{(-t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}\right)\right)\right]{\rm d}h^{\prime}{\rm d}h\,{\rm d}t^{\prime},

where,
(\hat{s}(t,h,t^{\prime},h^{\prime}),\hat{h}(t,h,t^{\prime},h^{\prime}))

are the coordinates of the intersection of the two radial birds with heads
(t,h)
and
(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})
and
\lambda_{\mathcal{R}}=\frac{4\sqrt{\lambda}}{\pi}
.

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

For all
(T_{i},H_{i})
and
(T_{j},H_{j})
in the support of
{\mathcal{H}}
, let

(\hat{S}_{i,j},\hat{H}_{i,j})
denote the coordinates of the intersection of the two radial birds with these heads.
By the very definition of
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathcal{R}}}^{0}
,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{R}}}^{0}[f(\theta_{\beta(0)}({\mathcal{H}}))]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{T_{i}\in{\mathcal{R}}\,\mbox{:}\,0\leq T_{i}\leq 1}f\left(\theta_{\beta(0)}\circ\theta_{T_{i}}({\mathcal{H}})\right)\right].

Using the property of the shift
\{\theta_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
, we have
\theta_{\beta(0)}\circ\theta_{T_{i}}=\theta_{T_{i}+\beta(T_{i})}
and hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{R}}}^{0}[f(\theta_{\beta(0)}({\mathcal{H}}))]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{T_{i}\in{\mathcal{R}},0\leq T_{i}\leq 1}f\left(\theta_{T_{i}+\beta(T_{i})}({\mathcal{H}})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{H}},0\leq T_{i}\leq 1}\mathbbmss{1}_{\exists(T_{j},H_{j})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,\mbox{:}\,T_{j}<T_{i}\mbox{ and }{\mathcal{H}}\left(U^{\hat{S}_{i,j}}_{\hat{H}_{i,j}}\right)=0}f\left(\theta_{T_{i}+\beta(T_{i})}({\mathcal{H}})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{H}},0\leq T_{i}\leq 1}\,\sum_{(T_{j},H_{j})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,\mbox{:}\,T_{j}<T_{i}}\mathbbmss{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}\left(U^{\hat{S}_{i,j}}_{\hat{H}_{i,j}}\right)=0}f\left(\theta_{T_{i}+\beta(T_{i})}({\mathcal{H}})\right)\right]

(5.4)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{H}},0\leq T_{i}\leq 1}\,\sum_{(T_{j},H_{j})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,\mbox{:}\,T_{j}<T_{i}}\mathbbmss{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}\left(U^{\hat{S}_{i,j}}_{\hat{H}_{i,j}}\right)=0}f\left(\theta_{\hat{S}_{i,j}}({\mathcal{H}})\right)\right].

Applying the Campbell-Mecke formula for the factorial power of order
2
of
{\mathcal{H}}
, we obtain from (
5.4
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{R}}}^{0}[f(\theta_{\beta(0)}({\mathcal{H}}))]

(5.5)

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\lambda)^{2}}{\lambda_{{\mathcal{R}}}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{(t,h),(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}_{\mathcal{H}}\left[\mathbbmss{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}\left(U^{\hat{s}(t,h,t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}_{\hat{h}(t,h,t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}\right)=0}f\left(\theta_{\hat{s}(t,h,t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}({\mathcal{H}})\right)\right]{\rm d}t^{\prime}{\rm d}h^{\prime}{\rm d}h{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{4\lambda^{2}}{\lambda_{{\mathcal{R}}}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{H}}\left[\mathbbmss{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}\left(U^{\hat{s}(t,h,t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}_{\hat{h}(t,h,t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}\right)=0}f\left(\theta_{\hat{s}(t,h,t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}({\mathcal{H}}+\delta_{(t,h)}+\delta_{(t^{\prime},h^{\prime})})\right)\right]{\rm d}t^{\prime}{\rm d}h^{\prime}{\rm d}h{\rm d}t{}

(5.6)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\lambda^{2}}{\lambda_{{\mathcal{R}}}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{t-t^{\prime}=\infty}^{0}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{H}}\left[\mathbbmss{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}\left(U^{\hat{s}(0,h,t^{\prime}-t,h^{\prime})}_{\hat{h}(0,h,t^{\prime}-t,h^{\prime})}\right)=0}f\left(\theta_{\hat{s}(0,h,t^{\prime}-t,h^{\prime})}({\mathcal{H}}+\delta_{(0,h)}+\delta_{(t^{\prime}-t,h^{\prime})})\right)\right]{\rm d}t^{\prime}{\rm d}h^{\prime}{\rm d}h{\rm d}t,

where we used the Slivnyak-Mecke formula for the last but one equation. We obtain the final result by the change of variable
t-t^{\prime}
to
t^{\prime}
in (
5.6
), similar to the one in (
4.26
).
∎

We hence get the following result using
\lambda_{\mathcal{R}}=\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}=\frac{4\sqrt{\lambda}}{\pi}
:

Theorem 5.4
.

For all measurable and non-negative
f

(5.7)

\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{0}[f({\mathcal{H}})]=\pi\lambda^{\frac{3}{2}}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{H}}\left[\mathbbmss{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}\left(U^{\hat{s}(0,h,-t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}_{\hat{h}(0,h,-t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}\right)=0}f\left(\theta_{\hat{s}(0,h,-t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}\left({\mathcal{H}}+\delta_{(0,h)}+\delta_{(-t^{\prime},h^{\prime})}\right)\right)\right]{\rm d}t^{\prime}{\rm d}h^{\prime}{\rm d}h.

Remark 5.5
.

Observe that the steps until (
5.5
) are true for any stationary point process on
\mathbb{H}^{+}
. In that sense Lemma
5.3
is far more general. The same holds true for Lemma
5.2
.

5.2.
Typical handover distance

Suppose
\hat{H}
is the random for distance of a typical handover. The following result evaluates the Laplace transform of
\hat{H}^{2}
under the Palm distribution
\mathbb{P}^{0}_{\mathcal{V}}
of
{\mathcal{V}}
based on Theorem
5.7
. This enables us to determine the distribution of
\hat{H}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

The Laplace transform of
\hat{H}^{2}
under the Palm probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{0}_{\mathcal{V}}
is

(5.8)

{\mathcal{L}}^{0}_{\hat{H}^{2}}(\gamma)=\mathbb{E}^{0}_{\mathcal{V}}\left[e^{-\gamma\hat{H}^{2}}\right]=\left(1+\frac{\gamma}{\lambda\pi}\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}},

for any
\gamma>0
. The squared handover-distance
\hat{H}^{2}
follows a Gamma distribution with parameters
\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{\lambda\pi}\right)
. Moreover, the handover distance
\hat{H}
follows a Nakagami distribution with parameters
\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2\lambda\pi}\right)
.

Proof of Lemma
5.6
.

In order to evaluate the Laplace transform of
\hat{H}^{2}
under the Palm probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{0}_{\mathcal{V}}
, we directly apply Theorem
5.7
, by taking
f({\mathcal{H}})=e^{-\gamma\hat{H}^{2}}
as follows:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{0}_{\hat{H}^{2}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{0}_{\mathcal{V}}\left[e^{-\gamma\hat{H}^{2}}\right]{}

\displaystyle=\pi\lambda^{3/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{H}}\left[\mathbbmss{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}\left(U^{\hat{s}}_{\hat{h}}\right)=0}e^{-\gamma(\hat{H}^{2}\circ\theta_{\hat{s}})}\right]{\rm d}t^{\prime}{\rm d}h^{\prime}{\rm d}h{}

(5.9)

\displaystyle=\pi\lambda^{3/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(\lambda\pi+\gamma)\hat{h}^{2}}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\,{\rm d}h^{\prime}\,{\rm d}h,

where
\hat{s}(0,h,-t^{\prime},h^{\prime})\equiv\hat{s}
and
\hat{h}(0,h,-t^{\prime},h^{\prime})\equiv\hat{h}
. The last step (
5.9
) is due to the fact that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{H}}\left[\mathbbmss{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}\left(U^{\hat{s}}_{\hat{h}}\right)=0}e^{-\gamma(\hat{H}^{2}\circ\theta_{\hat{s}})}\right]=e^{-\gamma\hat{h}^{2}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{H}}\left({\mathcal{H}}\left(U^{\hat{s}}_{\hat{h}}\right)=0\right)=e^{-\lambda\pi\hat{h}^{2}}e^{-\gamma\hat{h}^{2}},

as the handover distance
\hat{h}
does not change due to the shift
\theta_{\hat{s}}
. Recall from (
4.23
) that

(5.10)

\displaystyle\hat{h}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\left[t^{\prime 2}+\frac{(h^{2}-h^{\prime 2})^{2}}{t^{\prime 2}}+2(h^{2}+h^{\prime 2})\right].

Using the expression for
\hat{h}^{2}
, keeping aside the term
{\frac{1}{2}}(h^{2}+h^{\prime 2})
, we evaluate the innermost integral in (
5.9
), with respect to
t
, to be

(5.11)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi+\gamma}{4}\left[t^{\prime 2}+\frac{(h^{2}-h^{\prime 2})^{2}}{t^{\prime 2}}\right]}{\rm d}t^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\pi}{\lambda\pi+\gamma}\right)^{{\frac{1}{2}}}e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi+\gamma}{2}|h^{2}-h^{\prime 2}|},

evaluated using the definite integral
\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-ax^{2}-\frac{b}{x^{2}}}\,{\rm d}x=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2\sqrt{a}}e^{-2\sqrt{ab}}
from (
4.28
) with
a=\frac{\lambda\pi+\gamma}{4}>0
and
b=\frac{\lambda\pi+\gamma}{4}(h^{2}-h^{\prime 2})^{2}>0
. Substituting the resultant from (
5.11
) to (
5.9
) along with an extra factor of
e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi+\gamma}{2}(h^{2}+h^{\prime 2})}
, the right hand side in (
5.9
) turns out to be

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{0}_{\hat{H}^{2}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\pi\lambda^{3/2}\left(\frac{\pi}{\lambda\pi+\gamma}\right)^{{\frac{1}{2}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi+\gamma}{2}(h^{2}+h^{\prime 2})}e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi+\gamma}{2}|h^{2}-h^{\prime 2}|}{\rm d}h^{\prime}\,{\rm d}h{}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\pi^{3}\lambda^{3}}{\lambda\pi+\gamma}\right)^{{\frac{1}{2}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi+\gamma}{2}\left(h^{2}+h^{\prime 2}+|h^{2}-h^{\prime 2}|\right)}{\rm d}h^{\prime}\,{\rm d}h{}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\pi^{3}\lambda^{3}}{\lambda\pi+\gamma}\right)^{{\frac{1}{2}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(\lambda\pi+\gamma)\left(h^{2}\vee h^{\prime 2}\right)}{\rm d}h^{\prime}\,{\rm d}h{}

(5.12)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\pi^{3}\lambda^{3}}{\lambda\pi+\gamma}\right)^{{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{1}{\lambda\pi+\gamma}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(x^{2}\vee y^{2})}{\rm d}x\,{\rm d}y=\left(1+\frac{\gamma}{\lambda\pi}\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}},

as it can be shown that
\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(x^{2}\vee y^{2})}{\rm d}x\,{\rm d}y=1
. Hence it is clear that
\hat{H}^{2}
is distributed as a
\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{\lambda\pi}\right)
random variable and, as a result, the typical handover distance
\hat{H}
follows a Nakagami distribution with parameters
\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2\lambda\pi}\right)
. The probability density for
\hat{H}
is

(5.13)

f_{\hat{H}}(h)=4\pi\lambda^{3/2}h^{2}e^{-\lambda\pi h^{2}},\text{ for }h\geq 0,

and its mean is
\mathbb{E}[\hat{H}]=\frac{2}{\pi\sqrt{\lambda}}
.
∎

Remark 5.7

(Speed
v\neq 1
)
.

In the single-speed case with any non-unit station speed
v
, the typical handover distance
\hat{H}
also follows a Nakagami distribution with parameters
\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2\lambda\pi}\right)
, as the Laplace transform of
\hat{H}^{2}
, under the Palm probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{0}_{{\mathcal{V}}}
, remains the same, namely
{\mathcal{L}}^{0}_{\hat{H}^{2}}(\gamma)=\left(1+\frac{\gamma}{\lambda\pi}\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}}
. Indeed,

(5.14)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{0}_{\hat{H}^{2}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\lambda^{2}v^{2}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(\lambda\pi+\gamma)\hat{h}^{2}}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\,{\rm d}h^{\prime}\,{\rm d}h,

since the head point process
{\mathcal{H}}
has intensity
2\lambda v
, where
\hat{h}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\left[v^{2}t^{\prime 2}+\frac{(h^{2}-h^{\prime 2})^{2}}{v^{2}t^{\prime 2}}+2(h^{2}+h^{\prime 2})\right]
and
\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}=\frac{4v\sqrt{\lambda}}{\pi}
. Using this in (
5.14
) yields

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{0}_{\hat{H}^{2}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\pi\lambda^{3/2}v\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi+\gamma}{2}(h^{2}+h^{\prime 2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi+\gamma}{4}\left[v^{2}t^{\prime 2}+\frac{(h^{2}-h^{\prime 2})^{2}}{v^{2}t^{\prime 2}}\right]}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\,{\rm d}h^{\prime}\,{\rm d}h{}

\displaystyle=\pi\lambda^{3/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi+\gamma}{2}(h^{2}+h^{\prime 2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi+\gamma}{4}\left[t^{2}+\frac{(h^{2}-h^{\prime 2})^{2}}{t^{2}}\right]}{\rm d}t\,{\rm d}h^{\prime}\,{\rm d}h{}

\displaystyle=\pi\lambda^{3/2}\left(\frac{\pi}{\lambda\pi+\gamma}\right)^{{\frac{1}{2}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\lambda\pi+\gamma}{2}\left((h^{2}+h^{\prime 2})+|h^{2}-h^{\prime 2}|\right)}\,{\rm d}h^{\prime}\,{\rm d}h{}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\pi^{3}\lambda^{3}}{\lambda\pi+\gamma}\right)^{{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{1}{\lambda\pi+\gamma}=\left(1+\frac{\gamma}{\lambda\pi}\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}}{},

where the last two steps are as in (
5.12
).

5.3.
Comparison with other typical distances

In this section, we compare the statistical properties of the distance of the two stations involved in a typical handover already determined in Lemma
5.6
, the distance to a typical head (Lemma
5.11
) and the distance of the nearest station at a typical time (
5.20
).

5.3.1.
Distance to a typical head on
{\mathcal{L}}_{e}

Recall that,
{\mathcal{H}}:=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(T_{i},H_{i})}
be the head point process and suppose
{\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}\leq{\mathcal{H}}
, i.e.,
Supp({\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V})\subset Supp({\mathcal{H}})
, contains only those heads that lies on the lower envelope
{\mathcal{L}}_{e}
. In other words, we say that
{\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
is nothing but
{\mathcal{H}}
restricted to the lower envelope
{\mathcal{L}}_{e}
, i.e.,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}:={\mathcal{H}}|_{{\mathcal{L}}_{e}}
. We use the subscript
\scriptscriptstyle V
for the points being “visible from the time axis”, or equivalently being on the lower envelope
{\mathcal{L}}_{e}
. Let us write the point process
{\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
as

(5.15)

{\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}:=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(T^{\scriptscriptstyle V}_{j},H^{\scriptscriptstyle V}_{j})}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(T_{i},H_{i})}\mathbbmss{1}_{\{(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{L}}_{e}\}},

where we use the notation
(T^{\scriptscriptstyle V}_{j},H^{\scriptscriptstyle V}_{j})
for the visible head points. The sequence of points in
Supp({\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V})
gives rise to a stationary point process, say
{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
on
\mathbb{R}
, of the abscissas of the points in
Supp({\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V})
. In other words,

(5.16)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}:=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}\,\mbox{:}\,(T^{\scriptscriptstyle V}_{j},H^{\scriptscriptstyle V}_{j})\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}\delta_{T^{\scriptscriptstyle V}_{j}},

​​is the point process of the projections of the atoms of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
on the time axis. The point processes
{\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
time-stationarity from that of
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

The intensity of the point process
{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
is
\sqrt{\lambda}
.

Proof of Lemma
5.8
.

For
(s,h)\in\mathbb{H}^{+}
, the event that
\{(s,h)\text{ is an atom of }{\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}\}
is
equivalent to the event that
{\mathcal{H}}(U_{h}^{s})=0
. Thus the intensity of the point process
{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
, i.e.,
the expected number of points of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
in the unit interval
[0,1]
of the time axis is

\displaystyle\lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle V}=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{T^{\scriptscriptstyle V}_{j}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}\,\mbox{:}\,0\leq T^{\scriptscriptstyle V}_{j}\leq 1}1\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,\mbox{:}\,0\leq T_{i}\leq 1}\mathbbmss{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}(U^{T_{i}}_{H_{i}})=0}\right],{}

using (
5.15
). By applying the Campbell-Mecke formula for
{\mathcal{H}}
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle V}

\displaystyle=2\lambda\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-2\lambda|U_{h}^{t}|}\,{\rm d}h\,{\rm d}t=2\lambda\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\lambda\pi h^{2}}\,{\rm d}t\,{\rm d}h=2\lambda\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda\pi h^{2}}\,{\rm d}h=\sqrt{\lambda}.{}

∎

Remark 5.9
.

In the non-unit speed case
(v\neq 1)
, the intensity of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}

is
v\sqrt{\lambda}
. This result also recovers the assertion made in
[
7
, Theorem 3]
.

t
h
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

Figure 5.2
.
The mobile station, corresponding to the radial bird in red, partly serves the user, but not during the time when it is at its closest distance.

Remark 5.10
.

The point process
{\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
consists of only those atoms of
{\mathcal{H}}
that lie on the lower envelope.
There can be some atoms of
{\mathcal{H}}
for which a part of the corresponding radial bird contributes to the lower envelope,
but are such that the head point itself does not lie on
{\mathcal{L}}_{e}
, see Figure
5.2
.
In other words, the mobile stations corresponding to those points serve as the closest mobile station but not during the time when they are at their shortest distance to the user. Clearly, the point process

{\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
is not sufficient to provide the true handover frequency,
since
\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}=\frac{4\sqrt{\lambda}}{\pi}>\sqrt{\lambda}=\lambda_{V}
. As seen earlier, we certainly need more points to obtain
the complete picture of the handover point process
{\mathcal{V}}
.

One can define the Palm probability, denoted by
\mathbb{P}^{0}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}
, with respect to
{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
. Let
H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
be the distance of the typical head point corresponding to
{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
. This distance determines the best signal power received from the serving station, which is of practical interest.

Lemma 5.11
.

For any
\gamma\geq 0
, the Laplace transform of
H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{2}
, under the Palm probability distribution
\mathbb{P}^{0}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
, is given by

(5.17)

{\mathcal{L}}^{0}_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{2}}(\gamma)=\left(1+\frac{\gamma}{\lambda\pi}\right)^{-1/2},

that is,
H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
follows a Nakagami distribution with parameter
\left({\frac{1}{2}},\frac{1}{2\lambda\pi}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma\geq 0
. The Laplace transform of the random variable
H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{2}
, under the Palm probability distribution
\mathbb{P}^{0}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}
of the
{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
, is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{0}_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{2}}(\gamma):=\mathbb{E}^{0}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}\left[e^{-\gamma H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{(T^{\scriptscriptstyle V}_{j},H^{\scriptscriptstyle V}_{j})\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}\,\mbox{:}\,T^{\scriptscriptstyle V}_{j}\in[0,1]}e^{-\gamma\left(H^{\scriptscriptstyle V}_{j}\right)^{2}}\right]{}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,\mbox{:}\,T_{i}\in[0,1]}e^{-\gamma H_{i}^{2}}\mathbbmss{1}_{\{(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{L}}_{e}\}}\right]{}

(5.18)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{(T_{i},H_{i})\in{\mathcal{H}}\,\mbox{:}\,T_{i}\in[0,1]}e^{-\gamma H_{i}^{2}}\mathbbmss{1}_{\{{\mathcal{H}}(U^{T_{i}}_{H_{i}})=0\}}\right],

where we have used the fact that for any
(s,h)\in\mathbb{H}^{+}
,
\{(s,h)\in{\mathcal{L}}_{e}\}=\{{\mathcal{H}}(U^{s}_{h})=0\}
, by part (
i
) of Lemma
4.18
. Applying the Campbell-Mecke formula for the point process
{\mathcal{H}}
in (
5.18
), we get

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{0}_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{2}}(\gamma)=\frac{2\lambda}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}t\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\gamma h^{2}}e^{-\pi\lambda h^{2}}\,{\rm d}h

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(\pi\lambda+\gamma)h^{2}}\,{\rm d}h{}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x}\,\frac{1}{2\sqrt{x(\pi\lambda+\gamma)}}\,{\rm d}x{}

(5.19)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\lambda}{\pi\lambda+\gamma}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x}x^{-1/2}\,{\rm d}x=\left(1+\frac{\gamma}{\lambda\pi}\right)^{-{\frac{1}{2}}},

since
\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x}x^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}\,{\rm d}x=\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Thus
H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{2}
is distributed as a
\Gamma\left({\frac{1}{2}},\frac{1}{\lambda\pi}\right)
random variable and consequently
H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
follows a Nakagami distribution with parameter
\left({\frac{1}{2}},\frac{1}{2\lambda\pi}\right)
.
∎

5.3.2.
Distance of nearest station at a typical time

For any typical time, let
\tilde{H}
be the distance to the nearest station to user. Suppose the typical time is
t
, then we write
\tilde{H}\equiv\tilde{H}_{t}:=\inf_{i}||X_{i}^{t}||
, where
X_{i}\in\Phi
is the initial location of a station and
X_{i}^{t}
at time
t
. Then

(5.20)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\tilde{H}_{t}>h)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\inf_{i}||X_{i}^{t}||>h)=\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\Phi}^{t}(B_{h}(o))=0)=\mathbb{P}(\Phi(B_{h}(o))=0)=e^{-\lambda\pi h^{2}},

since
\tilde{\Phi}^{t}
and
\Phi
has the same distribution. So,
\tilde{H}
is distributed as Rayleigh with parameter
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\lambda\pi}}
.

5.3.3.
Laplace transform order

We say that two random variables
X,Y
are ordered for the Laplace transform order, i.e.,
X\leq_{Lt}Y
, if the corresponding Laplace transforms are ordered, i.e.,
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}(\gamma)\leq{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}(\gamma)
, for all
\gamma\geq 0
. The theory of Laplace transform order is studied in
[
26
]
.

Lemma 5.12
.

The typical distances
\hat{H}
,
\tilde{H}
and
H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
satisfy

(5.21)

\hat{H}\leq_{Lt}\tilde{H}\leq_{Lt}H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}.

Proof.

Recall the probability densities of the random variables
\hat{H}
,
\tilde{H}
and
H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}

f_{\hat{H}}(h)=4\pi\lambda^{3/2}h^{2}e^{-\lambda\pi h^{2}},f_{\tilde{H}}(h):=2\lambda\pi h\,e^{-\lambda\pi h^{2}}\text{ and }f_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(h)=2\lambda^{1/2}e^{-\lambda\pi h^{2}},\text{ for all }h\geq 0,

respectively. Observe that
f_{\tilde{H}}(h)\leq f_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(h)
on
[0,1/{\pi\sqrt{\lambda}}]
and
f_{\tilde{H}}(h)>f_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(h)
on
(1/{\pi\sqrt{\lambda}},\infty)
. In terms of Laplace transforms for all
\gamma\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(\gamma)-{\mathcal{L}}_{\tilde{H}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\gamma h}\left(f_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(h)-f_{\tilde{H}}(h)\right)\,{\rm d}h{}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1/{\pi\sqrt{\lambda}}}e^{-\gamma h}\left(f_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(h)-f_{\tilde{H}}(h)\right)\,{\rm d}h-\int_{1/{\pi\sqrt{\lambda}}}^{\infty}e^{-\gamma h}\left(f_{\tilde{H}}(h)-f_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(h)\right)\,{\rm d}h.{}

Using the value
h=\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{\lambda}}
gives a lower bound, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(\gamma)-{\mathcal{L}}_{\tilde{H}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle\geq e^{-\frac{\gamma}{\pi\sqrt{\lambda}}}\left[\int_{0}^{1/{\pi\sqrt{\lambda}}}\left(f_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(h)-f_{\tilde{H}}(h)\right)\,{\rm d}h-\int_{1/{\pi\sqrt{\lambda}}}^{\infty}\left(f_{\tilde{H}}(h)-f_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(h)\right)\,{\rm d}h\right]{}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{\gamma}{\pi\sqrt{\lambda}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(f_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(h)-f_{\hat{H}}(h)\right)\,{\rm d}h=0.{}

Thus
{\mathcal{L}}_{H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}(\gamma)\geq{\mathcal{L}}_{\tilde{H}}(\gamma)
for all
\gamma\geq 0
, which implies
\tilde{H}\leq_{Lt}H_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
. On the other hand, observe that
f_{\hat{H}}(h)\leq f_{\tilde{H}}(h)
on
[0,1/{2\sqrt{\lambda}}]
and
f_{\hat{H}}(h)>f_{\tilde{H}}(h)
on
(1/{2\sqrt{\lambda}},\infty)
. In terms of Laplace transforms, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\tilde{H}}(\gamma)-{\mathcal{L}}_{\hat{H}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\gamma h}\left(f_{\tilde{H}}(h)-f_{\hat{H}}(h)\right)\,{\rm d}h{}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1/{2\sqrt{\lambda}}}e^{-\gamma h}\left(f_{\tilde{H}}(h)-f_{\hat{H}}(h)\right)\,{\rm d}h-\int_{1/{2\sqrt{\lambda}}}^{\infty}e^{-\gamma h}\left(f_{\hat{H}}(h)-f_{\tilde{H}}(h)\right)\,{\rm d}h.{}

Using the value
h=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\lambda}}
again gives the lower bound

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\tilde{H}}(\gamma)-{\mathcal{L}}_{\hat{H}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle\geq e^{-\frac{\gamma}{2\sqrt{\lambda}}}\left[\int_{0}^{1/{2\sqrt{\lambda}}}\left(f_{\tilde{H}}(h)-f_{\hat{H}}(h)\right)\,{\rm d}h-\int_{1/{2\sqrt{\lambda}}}^{\infty}\left(f_{\hat{H}}(h)-f_{\tilde{H}}(h)\right)\,{\rm d}h\right]{}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{\gamma}{2\sqrt{\lambda}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(f_{\tilde{H}}(h)-f_{\hat{H}}(h)\right)\,{\rm d}h=0.{}

Thus
{\mathcal{L}}_{\tilde{H}}(\gamma)\geq{\mathcal{L}}_{\hat{H}}(\gamma)
for all
\gamma\geq 0
, which implies
\hat{H}\leq_{Lt}\tilde{H}
. The completes the proof of the Laplace transform ordering (
5.21
) among the typical distances.
∎

5.4.
Distribution of inter-handover time

Let us formally define the inter-handover time under Palm in terms of the head point process
{\mathcal{H}}
. For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote the first time a handovers happens after time
s
as
T_{1}(s)
. Suppose there is a handover at time
0
given by the intersection of two radial birds
C_{(t_{1},h_{1})}
and
C_{(t_{2},h_{2})}
, for some
(t_{1},h_{1})
and
(t_{2},h_{2})
such that
t_{2}\leq t_{1}
. Let
T
denotes the inter-handover time, which coincides with
T_{1}(0)
. Then the next handover will be given by an intersection that happens on the part of the radial bird
C_{(t_{1},h_{1})}
lying on the right of the vertical line
t=0
.

Consider the sequence
\{U^{t}_{\hat{h}_{t}}\}_{t\geq 0}
of open half-balls of heights
\{\hat{h}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, where

\hat{h}_{t}:=\left((t-t_{1})^{2}+h_{1}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Let us also consider the collection
\left\{S_{t}\right\}_{t\geq 0}
, where

(5.22)

S_{t}:={\kern 0.0pt\left(\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}U^{t^{\prime}}_{\hat{h}_{t^{\prime}}}\setminus U^{0}_{\hat{h}_{0}}\right)}^{\mathrm{o}}.

We claim that the collection
\left\{S_{t}\right\}_{t\geq 0}
of open sets is increasing in
t
, using the following Lemma
5.13
, for which we provide a geometric proof in Appendix
A.2
.

Lemma 5.13
.

The collection of sets
\left\{U^{t}_{\hat{h}_{t}}\setminus U^{0}_{\hat{h}_{0}}\right\}_{t\geq 0}
is increasing in
t
.

Remark 5.14

(Increasing wing property)
.

In general, we call the decreasing and the increasing branches of the curve of a radial bird as its
decreasing wing
and
increasing wing
, respectively.
Suppose
(t_{1},h_{1})\in\mathbb{H}^{+}
with
t_{1}\geq 0
, such that there is a radial bird
C_{(t_{1},h_{1})}
with head at
(t_{1},h_{1})
and
(0,\hat{h}_{0})\in C_{(t_{1},h_{1})}
. Then the collection
\left\{U^{t}_{\hat{h}_{t}}\setminus U^{0}_{\hat{h}_{0}}\right\}_{t\geq t_{1}}
is non-decreasing, where
\hat{h}_{t}=\left((t-t_{1})^{2}+h_{1}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, since the point
(t,\hat{h}_{t})
lies on the increasing wing of the radial bird at
(t_{1},h_{1})
. We often refer to this property as the
increasing wing property
for the single speed case.

Using the increasing property of the collection
\left\{U^{t}_{\hat{h}_{t}}\setminus U^{0}_{\hat{h}_{0}}\right\}_{t\geq 0}
, as in Lemma
5.13
, we have

S_{t}={\kern 0.0pt\Big(\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}U^{t^{\prime}}_{\hat{h}_{t^{\prime}}}\setminus U^{0}_{\hat{h}_{0}}\Big)}^{\mathrm{o}}=U^{t}_{\hat{h}_{t}}\setminus\overline{U^{0}_{\hat{h}_{0}}}.

Also observe that the collection of sets
\left\{S_{t}\right\}_{t\geq 0}
lies entirely in the region
Q_{0}\setminus\overline{U^{0}_{\hat{h}_{0}}}
, where
Q_{s}:=\left\{(t,h)\in\mathbb{H}^{+}\,\mbox{:}\,t\geq s\right\}
is the right quadrant with respect to the vertical line
t=s
.

Definition 5.15

(Inter-handover time)
.

Given that there is a handover at time
0
, the time taken for the next handover is defined as

(5.23)

T:=\inf\left\{s>0:\forall t\in(0,s],{\mathcal{H}}\left(S_{t}\right)=0,\text{ but }{\mathcal{H}}\left(\overline{S_{s}}\right)=2\right\},

which is equivalent to
T_{1}(0)
under the handover Palm probability distribution.

As a consequence of the increasing property of the sets
\left\{S_{t}\right\}_{t>0}
, we can conclude that
{\mathcal{H}}\left(S_{T_{1}(0)}\right)=0
, implies
{\mathcal{H}}\left(S_{t}\right)=0
, for all
t\in[0,T_{1}(0)]
. Additionally
{\mathcal{H}}\left(\overline{S_{T_{1}(0)}}\right)=2
. This essentially implies there exists a third head point
(t_{3},h_{3})
such that
C_{(t_{1},h_{1})}
and
C_{(t_{3},h_{3})}
intersect at
\left(T_{1}(0),\hat{h}_{T_{1}(0)}\right)
,
{\mathcal{H}}\left(S_{T_{1}(0)}\right)=0
and
{\mathcal{H}}\left(\overline{S_{T_{1}(0)}}\right)=2
, see Figure
5.3
. Hence we have the following corollary of Lemma
5.13
:

t
(0,0)
(T_{1}(0),0)
\bullet
(t_{1},h_{1})
h
\hat{h}_{0}
\hat{h}_{T_{1}(0)}
U_{\hat{h}_{T_{1}(0)}}^{T_{1}(0)}
U^{0}_{\hat{h}_{0}}
\bullet
(t_{2},h_{2})
\bullet
(t_{3},h_{3})

Figure 5.3
.
The sequence of increasing sets of the form
S_{t}={\kern 0.0pt(U^{t}_{\hat{h}_{t}}\setminus U^{0}_{\hat{h}_{0}})}^{\mathrm{o}}
has no points of
{\mathcal{H}}
, for all
t\in[0,T_{1}(0)]
, we just draw a two of them in red and blue. Here
{\mathcal{H}}\left(\overline{S_{T_{1}(0)}}\right)=2
.

t
\bullet
(t_{1},h_{1})
t=t_{1}

(T_{1}(0),0)

U^{0}_{\hat{h}_{0}}
